Frequently asked questions Appendix C - Defined terms Property, plant and equipment Overview IAS 16 Property, plant and equipment prescribes the accounting treatment for property, plant and equipment (PP&E). The principal issues in accounting for PP&E are determining the appropriate initial recognition and cost of assets, subsequent measurement of carrying amounts and depreciation charges and impairment losses to be recognised in relation to them. [IAS 16.1]. IAS 16 also prescribes the disclosures that an entity should provide about PP&E. This chapter discusses how IAS 16 is applied, and covers important aspects of the standard, including the recognition of PP&E, measurement at recognition, measurement after recognition under the cost model and under the revaluation model respectively, depreciation, derecognition and disposal of PP&E, as well as the disclosure requirements. Impairment is a major consideration in accounting for PP&E, as this is intended to ensure PP&E costs that are not fully recoverable are immediately written down to a level that is recoverable. Impairment is covered by IAS 36 Impairment of Assets and dealt with as a separate topic in Chapter 21. In addition, there is a separate standard, IAS 40 Investment Property that deals with that particular category of PP&E which is discussed in Chapter 20. IFRS 5 Non-current Assets Held for Sale and Discontinued Operations deals with the accounting required when items of PP&E are held for sale and is discussed in Chapter 5. What you need to know • IAS 16 applies to all property, plant and equipment, unless explicitly excluded from its scope. • Other standards may require items similar to PP&E to be recognised on a different basis from that required by IAS 16 (e.g., IFRS 16 Leases which prescribes the accounting treatment for right-of- use assets). • The standard allows one of two alternatives to be chosen as the accounting policy for measurement of PP&E after initial recognition: • The cost model Or • The revaluation model The choice must be applied to an entire class of PP&E. Property, plant and equipment Introduction One fundamental topic in financial reporting is how to account periodically for performance when many of the expenditures an entity incurs in the current period also contribute to that in future accounting periods. Expenditure on property, plant and equipment (‘PP&E’) is among the best examples of this issue. The accounting conventions permitted by the IASB under IAS 16 are the subject of this chapter, with the underlying broad principles involved being among the first that accountants learn in their professional life. The cost of an item of PP&E is capitalised when acquired (i.e., recorded in the statement of financial position as an asset); then subsequently a proportion of the cost is charged each year to profit or loss (i.e., the cost is spread over the future accounting periods expected to benefit from the item). After initial recognition, entities have an accounting policy choice for measurement of PP&E, applying either the cost model or the revaluation model (by class of asset). Ideally, at the end of the item’s working life the carrying amount remaining on the statement of financial position should be equal to the disposal proceeds of the item or be zero if there are none. The principal standard is IAS 16. The objective of this standard is to prescribe the accounting treatment for PP&E so that users of the financial statements can discern information about an entity’s investment in its PP&E and the changes in such investment over time. The principal issues in accounting for PP&E are the recognition of the assets, the determination of their carrying amounts and the depreciation charges and impairment losses to be recognised in relation to them. [IAS 16.1]. FASB differences Differences between the IFRS and US GAAP guidance on this topic are generally not addressed in this chapter as the standards are not converged. Therefore, differences between IFRS and US GAAP may exist that are not discussed in this chapter. For more information, refer to our US GAAP/IFRS accounting differences identifier tool, which helps entities to identify some of the more common accounting differences between US GAAP and IFRS. Property, plant and equipment The requirements of IAS 16 2.1 Scope IAS 16 defines PP&E as tangible items that are held for use in the production or supply of goods or services, for rental to others, or for administrative purposes and are expected to be used during more than one period. [IAS 16.6]. All PP&E is within the scope of IAS 16 except as follows: • When another standard requires or permits a different accounting treatment, for example, IAS 40 for investment properties held at fair value (but investment properties held using the cost model under IAS 40 should use the cost model in IAS 16 which is discussed in section 5 below) • PP&E classified as held for sale in accordance with IFRS 5 • Biological assets related to agricultural activity (covered by IAS 41 Agriculture) other than bearer plants (see section 3.1.7 below) • The recognition and measurement of exploration and evaluation assets (covered by IFRS 6 Exploration for and Evaluation of Mineral Resources) • Mineral rights and mineral reserves such as oil, natural gas, and similar ‘non-regenerative’ resources [IAS 16.2, IAS 16.3, IAS 16.5] Although the standard scopes out non-bearer plant biological assets, exploration and evaluation assets and mineral rights and reserves, it includes any PP&E used in developing or maintaining such resources. [IAS 16.3]. Therefore, PP&E used in exploration activities is included in the scope of the standard (see # Chapter 39), as is agricultural PP&E (see Chapter 38). The accounting treatment of collectibles, for example paintings or sculptures, depends on the purpose for which the entity is holding the collectibles. Items collected for administrative or aesthetic purposes and not traded in the ordinary course of business meet the definition of an item of property, plant, and equipment in IAS 16. [IAS 16.6]. For further discussion see Chapter 23 at section 2.3.1.J. Other standards may require an item of PP&E to be recognised on a basis different from that required by IAS 16. For example under IFRS 16, lessees will recognise most leases in their statement of financial position as lease liabilities with corresponding right-of-use assets (see section 8.4 below). Consequently, accounting for right-of-use assets should be in accordance with IFRS 16 (see Chapter 24). Property, plant and equipment 2.2 Definitions used in IAS 16 IAS 16 defines the main terms it uses throughout the standard as follows: [IAS 16.6] Extract from IAS 16 Definitions The following terms are used in this Standard with the meanings specified: A bearer plant is a living plant that: (a) is used in the production or supply of agricultural produce; (b) is expected to bear produce for more than one period; and (c) has a remote likelihood of being sold as agricultural produce, except for incidental scrap sales. (Paragraphs 5A-5B of IAS 41 elaborate on this definition of a bearer plant.) Carrying amount is the amount at which an asset is recognised after deducting any accumulated depreciation and accumulated impairment losses. Cost is the amount of cash or cash equivalents paid or the fair value of the other consideration given to acquire an asset at the time of its acquisition or construction or, where applicable, the amount attributed to that asset when initially recognised in accordance with the specific requirements of other IFRSs, eg IFRS 2 Share-based Payment. Depreciable amount is the cost of an asset, or other amount substituted for cost, less its residual value. Depreciation is the systematic allocation of the depreciable amount of an asset over its useful life. Entity-specific value is the present value of the cash flows an entity expects to arise from the continuing use of an asset and from its disposal at the end of its useful life or expects to incur when settling a liability. Fair value is the price that would be received to sell an asset or paid to transfer a liability in an orderly transaction between market participants at the measurement date. (See IFRS 13 Fair Value Measurement.) An impairment loss is the amount by which the carrying amount of an asset exceeds its recoverable amount. Property, plant and equipment are tangible items that: (a) are held for use in the production or supply of goods or services, for rental to others, or for administrative purposes; and (b) are expected to be used during more than one period. Recoverable amount is the higher of an asset’s fair value less costs of disposal and its value in use. The residual value of an asset is the estimated amount that an entity would currently obtain from disposal of the asset, after deducting the estimated costs of disposal, if the asset were already of the age and in the condition expected at the end of its useful life. Useful life is: (a) the period over which an asset is expected to be available for use by an entity; or (b) the number of production or similar units expected to be obtained from the asset by an entity. These definitions are discussed in the relevant sections below. Property, plant and equipment Recognition An item of PP&E should be recognised (i.e., its cost included as an asset in the statement of financial position) only if it is probable that future economic benefits associated with the item will flow to the entity and its cost can be measured reliably. [IAS 16.7]. Practical example 3-1 below describes Skanska AB’s criteria for the recognition of PP&E, as disclosed in their 2022 Annual and Sustainability Report. Practical example 3-1: Skanska AB (2022) Sweden Annual and Sustainability Report 2022 [extract] Financial information [extract] Notes including accounting and valuation principles [extract] Note 1. Consolidated accounting and valuation principles [extract] IAS 16 Property, Plant and Equipment [extract] ## Contents - 8. Non-current assets [extract] Property, plant and equipment 3.1 Aspects of recognition 3.1.1 Spare parts and minor items Items such as spare parts, stand-by equipment and servicing equipment are inventory unless they meet the definition of PP&E (see section 2.2 above). [IAS 16.8]. This treatment is illustrated in Practical example 3-2 below. Practical example 3-2: Heineken N.V. (2023) The Netherlands Annual Report 2023 [extract] Financial Statements 2023 [extract] Notes to the Consolidated Financial Statements [extract] ### Property, plant and equipment [extract] Accounting policies [extract] … Materiality judgements are considered when deciding how an item of PP&E should be accounted for. Major spare parts may, for example, be considered as qualifying as PP&E, while smaller spares may be carried as inventory and as a practical matter many companies have a minimum value for capitalising assets. Some types of business may have a very large number of minor items of PP&E such as spare parts, tools, pallets and returnable containers, which nevertheless are used in more than one accounting period. There are practical problems in recording them on an asset-by-asset basis in an asset register; they are difficult to control and frequently lost. The main consequence is that it becomes very difficult to depreciate them. Generally, entities write off such immaterial assets as expenses in the period of addition. Skanska AB in Practical example 5-1 (see section 5.2 below) immediately depreciates such minor equipment, achieving the same result. The standard notes that there are issues concerning what constitutes a single item of PP&E. The ‘unit of measure’ for recognition is not prescribed and entities have to apply judgement in defining an item of PP&E in their specific circumstances. The standard suggests that it may be appropriate to aggregate individually insignificant items (such as tools, moulds and dies) and to apply the standard to the aggregate amount (presumably without having to identify the individual assets). [IAS 16.9]. 3.1.2 Environmental and safety equipment The standard acknowledges that there may be expenditures forced upon an entity by legislation that requires it to buy items of PP&E that do not directly increase the future economic benefits expected to flow to the entity. Examples would be safety or environmental protection equipment. IAS 16 explains that these expenditures qualify for recognition as they allow an entity to derive future economic benefits from related assets in excess of those that would flow if such expenditure had not been made. For example, a chemical manufacturer may install new chemical handling processes to comply with environmental requirements for the production and storage of dangerous chemicals; related plant enhancements are recognised as an asset because without them the entity is unable to manufacture and sell the chemicals or a plant might have to be closed down if these safety or environmental expenditures were not made. [IAS 16.11]. Property, plant and equipment How we see it An entity may voluntarily invest in environmental equipment even though it is not required by law to do so. Although not explicitly stated in IAS 16, we believe the entity can capitalise those investments in environmental and safety equipment in the absence of a legal requirement as long as: • The expenditure meets the definition of an asset. Or • There is a constructive obligation to invest in the equipment. If the entity can demonstrate that the equipment is likely to increase the economic life of the related asset, or enables the entity to derive additional economic benefit from other assets, the expenditure meets the definition of an asset. Otherwise, the expenditure can be capitalised when the entity can demonstrate all of the following: • That a constructive obligation exists to invest in environmental and safety equipment (e.g., it is standard practice in the industry, environmental groups are likely to raise issues or employees demand certain equipment to be present). • The expenditure is directly related to improvement of the asset’s environmental and safety standards. And • The expenditure is not related to repairs and maintenance or form part of period costs or operational costs. Whenever safety and environmental assets are capitalised, the standard requires the resulting carrying amount of the asset, and any related asset, to be reviewed for impairment in accordance with IAS 36 (see section 5.7 below). [IAS 16.11]. 3.1.3 Economic benefits and costs associated with property developments The standard requires that PP&E only be recognised when it is probable that future economic benefits associated with the item will flow to the entity. [IAS 16.7]. For example, in relation to property development, many jurisdictions require permissions prior to development whilst developers, including entities developing property for their own use, typically incur significant costs prior to such permissions being granted. In assessing whether such pre-permission expenditures can be capitalised - assuming they otherwise meet the criteria - a judgement must be made at the date the expenditure is incurred of whether it is sufficiently probable that the relevant permission will be granted. Such expenditure does not become part of the cost of the land; to the extent that it can be recognised it is part of the costs of a separate building. How we see it If during the application and approval process of such permits it is no longer expected that necessary permits will be granted, capitalisation of pre-permission expenditure should cease, any related amounts that were previously capitalised should be written off in accordance with IAS 36 and accordingly, the carrying amount of any related item of PP&E subject to development or redevelopment (or, if appropriate, the cash generating unit where such an asset belongs) should be tested for impairment, where applicable (see section 5.7 below). 3.1.4 Classification as PP&E or intangible asset Certain intangible assets may be embedded or contained within an item of PP&E, and this can raise questions in determining whether such assets should be accounted for under IAS 16 or IAS 38 Intangible Assets. In practice, items such as computer software purchased by entities are frequently capitalised as part of a tangible asset, for example as part of an accounting or communications infrastructure. Equally, internally written software may be capitalised as part of a tangible production facility, and so on. Judgement must be exercised in deciding whether such items are to be accounted for under IAS 16 or IAS 38 and this distinction becomes increasingly important if the two standards prescribe differing treatments in any particular case. Property, plant and equipment IAS 16 does not refer to this type of asset. In contrast, IAS 38 contains explicit guidance, stating that where an asset incorporates both intangible and tangible elements, an entity needs to exercise judgement to assess which element is more significant in order to determine whether the asset should be treated as PP&E under IAS 16 or as an intangible asset under IAS 38, for example: • Computer software that is embedded in computer-controlled equipment that cannot operate without that specific software is an integral part of the related hardware and is treated as PP&E. • Software which is not an integral part of the related hardware is treated as an intangible asset. For example, application software that is being used on a computer is generally easily replaced and is not an integral part of the related hardware. In contrast, the operating system normally is integral to the computer and is included in PP&E. • A database that is stored on a compact disc is considered to be an intangible asset because the value of the physical medium is wholly insignificant compared to that of the data collection. [IAS 38.4]. It is worthwhile noting that as the ‘parts approach’ in IAS 16 requires an entity to account for significant parts of an asset separately, this raises ‘boundary’ problems between IAS 16 and IAS 38 when software and similar expenditure are involved. How we see it We believe that where IAS 16 requires an entity to identify significant parts of an asset and account for them separately, the entity needs to evaluate whether any software-type intangible part is integral to the larger asset or whether it is really a separate asset in its own right. The intangible part is more likely to be an asset in its own right if it was developed separately or if it can be used independently of the item of PP&E. 3.1.5 Classification of items as inventory or PP&E when minimum levels are maintained Entities may acquire items of inventory on a continuing basis, either for sale in the ordinary course of business or to be consumed in a production process or when rendering services. This means there will always be a core stock of that item (i.e., a minimum level of inventory is maintained). This does not in itself turn that inventory into an item of PP&E since each individual item will be consumed within a single operating cycle. However, there may be cases where it is difficult to judge whether an item is part of inventory or is an item of PP&E. This may have implications on measurement because, for example, PP&E has a revaluation option (see section 6 below) that is not available for inventory. How we see it In our view, an item of inventory is accounted for as an item of PP&E if it: • Is not held for sale in the ordinary course of business or consumed in a production process or during the process of rendering services • Is necessary to operate or benefit from another asset during more than one operating cycle And • Either cannot be recouped through sale (or is significantly impaired after it has been used to operate the asset or benefit from that asset) This applies even if the part of inventory that is an item of PP&E cannot be physically separated from the rest of inventories. Consider the following examples: • An entity acquires the right to use an underground cave for gas storage purposes for a period of 50 years. The cave is filled with gas, but a substantial part (25%) of that gas will only be used to keep the cave under pressure in order to be able to get gas out of the cave. It is not possible to distinguish the gas that will be used to keep the cave under pressure from the rest of the gas. Gas will be turned around on a continuing basis. Still, it is possible to measure the part of the gas that will not be recoupable from the cave for selling purposes. • An entity operates an oil refining plant. In order for the refining process to take place, the plant must contain a certain minimum quantity of oil. This can only be taken out once the plant is abandoned and would then be polluted to such an extent that the oil’s value is significantly reduced (it can only be sold as sludge). • An entity sells gas and has at any one time a certain quantity of gas in its gas distribution network. Property, plant and equipment In the first example the total volume of gas must be virtually split into (i) gas held for sale and (ii) gas held to keep the cave under pressure. The former must be accounted for under IAS 2 Inventories. The latter must be accounted for as PP&E and depreciated over the period the cave is expected to be used. In the second example the part of the crude that is necessary to operate (in technical terms) the plant and cannot be recouped (or can be recouped but would then be significantly impaired), even when the plant is abandoned, should be considered as an item of PP&E and amortised over the life of the plant. The arguments for the conclusions in these two examples are that the items do not meet the definition of inventories, and although it is not possible to identify the molecules in each of the two categories, there is no reason why one should not distinguish assets with a different use, and account for them differently. Further, the items are necessary to bring another item of PP&E to the condition necessary for it to be capable of operating in the manner intended by management and this meets the definition of cost of an item of PP&E in IAS 16.16(b). Recognising these items as inventories would lead to an immediate loss because these items cannot be sold or consumed in a production process or during the process of rendering services. This does not properly reflect the fact that the items are necessary to operate another asset over more than one operating cycle. [IAS 2.6, IAS 16.6, IAS 16.16(b)]. In the third example the gas in the pipeline is not necessary to operate the pipeline. It is held for sale or to be consumed in the production process or process of rendering services. Therefore, this gas is accounted for as inventory. 3.1.6 Production stripping costs of surface mines IFRIC 20 Stripping Costs in the Production Phase of a Surface Mine states that costs associated with a ‘stripping activity asset’ (i.e., the costs associated with gaining access to a specific section of an ore body) are accounted for as an additional component of an existing asset. [IFRIC 20.10]. Other routine stripping costs are accounted for as current costs of production (i.e., inventory). The IFRS IC’s intention was to maintain the principle of IAS 16 by requiring identification of the component of the ore body for which access had been improved, as part of the criteria for recognising stripping costs as an asset. An entity will have to allocate the stripping costs between the amount capitalised (as it reflects the future access benefit) and the amount that relates to the current-period production of inventory. This allocation should be based on a relevant production measure. [IFRIC 20.13]. This component approach follows the principle of separating out parts of an asset that have costs that are significant in relation to the entire asset and when the useful lives of those parts are different. [IAS 16.43, **IAS 16.44, IAS 16.45].** This interpretation is discussed in more detail in Chapter 39 at section 15.5. 3.1.7 Bearer plants Bearer plants are defined as living plants that are used in the production or supply of agricultural produce, are expected to bear produce for more than one period and have a remote likelihood of being sold as a plant or harvested as agricultural produce (except for incidental scrap sales such as for use as firewood). [IAS 16.6, IAS 41.5, IAS 41.5B]. Examples of bearer plants may include tea bushes, grape vines, oil palms and rubber trees. Bearer plants are within the scope of IAS 16 and subject to all of the requirements therein. This includes the ability to choose between the cost model and revaluation model for subsequent measurement. Agricultural produce growing on bearer plants, e.g., the fruit growing on a tree, remains within the scope of IAS 41 (see # Chapter 38). [IAS 16.3(b), IAS 41.5C]. The following are not included within the definition of bearer plants and instead fall under the scope of IAS 41: • Plants cultivated to be harvested as agricultural produce, e.g., trees grown for use as lumber. • Plants cultivated to produce agricultural produce when there is more than a remote likelihood that the entity will also harvest and sell the plant as agricultural produce, other than as incidental scrap sales, e.g., trees that are cultivated both for their fruit and their lumber. • Annual crops such as maize and wheat. [IAS 41.5A]. Bearer plants are accounted for in the same way as self-constructed items of PP&E before they are brought to the location and condition necessary to be capable of operating in the manner intended by management. Consequently, references to ‘construction’ in IAS 16, with respect to bearer plants, cover the activities that are necessary to cultivate such plants before they are brought to the location and condition necessary to be capable of operating in the manner intended by management, which is typically when they are mature. [IAS 16.22A]. Property, plant and equipment Bearer plants are subject to the requirements of IAS 16, and so entities will need to consider the correct unit of account, analyse which costs can be capitalised prior to maturity, set useful lives for depreciation purposes and consider the possibility of impairment. For a more detailed discussion of the requirements, including some of the measurement challenges for bearer plants under IAS 16, see Chapter 38 at sections 1, 2.2.1.A, 2.3.3, and 3.2.3.A. 3.1.8 Accounting for trees held to generate carbon offsets for use or sale As part of their sustainability related activities, some entities are buying or planting trees that will absorb carbon dioxide (i.e., act as ‘carbon sinks’). Others are choosing to defer or cease planned harvests to generate carbon offsets. The entities undertaking these activities can then sell or use the carbon offsets generated. An entity that directly owns such trees (rather than indirectly through investments in entities that own the trees) will need to determine whether the trees are: • Biological assets related to agricultural activity to which IAS 41 applies • Bearer plants related to agricultural activity to which IAS 16 applies Or • Assets not related to agricultural activity, in which case, it is likely an entity would apply the requirements in IAS 16. Determining the nature of the asset is fundamental to this assessment. To be within the scope of IAS 41 or to be a bearer plant within the scope of IAS 16, the trees must relate to agricultural activity, which is defined as “the management by an entity of the biological transformation and harvest of biological assets for sale or for conversion into agricultural produce or into additional biological assets”. [IAS 41.1(a), IAS 41.5]. For the definition of agricultural activity in IAS 41 to be met, both the management of change (which may include activities such as planting, pruning, thinning, and fertilising) and the harvest of agricultural produce are required. As such, whether the entity manages the trees and harvests any agricultural produce will be key to determining the nature of the asset and the appropriate standard to apply. How we see it While carbon dioxide is absorbed and oxygen is produced by the trees, the oxygen cannot be captured at the point of production and detached in the manner contemplated by the definition of agricultural produce in IAS 41. Similarly, in our view, carbon offsets are not the agricultural produce of trees. IAS 41 does not provide guidance on the extent of harvest needed to qualify as an agricultural activity. As a result, it may not always be clear the extent to which any felling undertaken might constitute harvesting of agricultural produce and entities will need to apply judgement in determining whether the trees are engaged in agricultural activity. Further guidance on assessing the nature of the asset in such cases is provided at Chapter 38 at section 2.3.7. Where it is determined that the trees are bearer plants, the requirements at section 3.1.7 above would apply. Where it is determined that the trees are not held in relation to agricultural activity, it is likely an entity would apply the general requirements in IAS 16. How we see it If the trees are held solely to generate carbon offsets, whether these are sold or used by the entity, determining the nature of the asset may require judgement. After initial recognition, it is not clear whether reassessment might be required should circumstances change. For example, assume an entity had no intention of felling the trees, but that subsequently changed for some of the trees due to windthrow. If a change in circumstances were to arise, an entity would need to apply the hierarchy in IAS 8 Accounting Policies, Changes in Accounting Estimates and Errors to develop an appropriate policy. If, having assessed the nature of the assets, it is determined that IAS 16 applies and the asset is not a bearer plant, the assets shall be initially measured at their accumulated cost until they are available for use, which, in contrast to the treatment outlined above for bearer plants, will generally be as soon as they are planted. Property, plant and equipment 3.1.9 Expenditure on the development of infrastructure relating to new technologies An entity may incur expenditure on the development of infrastructure relating to new technologies. One instance in which this may be particularly relevant is those entities incurring expenditure arising from the need to address impacts of climate change (e.g., hydrogen processing or carbon capture and storage (CCS) facilities). Such development costs are recognised as an item of PP&E if it is probable that future economic benefits associated with the item will flow to the entity and the cost of the item can be measured reliably. The entity will need to consider at which point there is sufficient and reliable information to meet the PP&E recognition criteria. This is key as there will likely be more uncertainty about the total project costs of assets relating to new technologies compared to existing technologies. Therefore, there is greater potential for significant time and cost overruns. 3.1.10 Aggregation of items of PP&E Entities may have items of PP&E that are considered insignificant at group level because the group is of such a size that the individual assets are considered insignificant to the group as a whole. As described in section 3.1.1 above, IAS 16 does not prescribe the ‘unit of measure’ for recognition, i.e., what constitutes an item of PP&E and the extent to which items can be aggregated. [IAS 16.9]. The standard notes that it may be appropriate to aggregate individually insignificant items such as moulds, tools and dies, and to apply the standard to the aggregate value, but is silent on whether this approach may be used to aggregate properties or similar assets that are individually immaterial. There are instances in which the aggregation of items on the grounds that they are immaterial to the entity purely by virtue of their size may result in accounting that is inconsistent with the broader requirements of IAS 16. For example, the aggregation of individually immaterial assets into a single asset could lead to a factory being considered a single asset, instead of distinguishing between the land, buildings, plant and machinery, which would clearly be against both the letter and spirit of IAS 16. The following Illustration shows another situation: Illustration 3-1: Aggregation of individual properties An entity owns a large number of individual properties from which it earns both rental income and other trading income, such that the properties do not meet the definition of investment properties under IAS 40. The entity applies the revaluation model for subsequent measurement of its properties. Each property is individually immaterial to the entity. Nevertheless, each property is recorded separately in the entity’s books and records, is monitored separately by management and generates individual cash flows. Therefore, each individual property is regarded as a separate item of PP&E under IAS 16. If such an entity did aggregate all its properties, any downward revaluations on individual properties to the income statement would be inappropriately offset by revaluation gains arising on other properties within the aggregated group. How we see it Paragraph 9 of IAS 16 applies to small items (such as tools) that are capitalised together into a single amount and are not subsequently viewed by management or monitored as single assets. In our view, the paragraph cannot be used to justify the aggregation of individual assets if the entity itself views and monitors the assets individually, the assets generate separately identifiable cashflows or the assets are subject to separate contractual arrangements. 3.2 Accounting for parts (‘components’) of assets IAS 16 requires ‘significant parts’ of an asset to be depreciated separately. These are parts that have a cost that is significant in relation to the total cost of the asset. Therefore, on initial recognition, an entity will have to identify the significant parts of the asset in order to depreciate each part of the asset properly. [IAS 16.43, IAS 16.44]. There is no requirement to identify all parts. The process of identifying and separately recognising the major parts of an item PP&E is often referred to as ‘componentisation’. ‘Parts’ are distinguished from day-to-day servicing, but they are not otherwise defined by the standard; moreover, as described in section 3.1.1, the unit of measurement to which the standard applies (i.e., what comprises an item of PP&E) is not itself defined. Property, plant and equipment Components may be physical components, but where relevant, non-physical parts may also be identified to reflect the need for the asset to undergo major inspections or overhauls. Major inspections are discussed further in section 3.3.2 below. The separation of an asset into major physical components is illustrated in illustration 3-2: Illustration 3-2: Identification of component parts An entity buys a property with an estimated useful life of 25 years for CU4 million. The property’s roof is expected to require replacement every 10 years at an estimated cost of CU250,000. The building is also internally configured using partition walls in order to create the required workspaces (such as open plan areas and meeting rooms), and this internal configuration is expected to be replaced every 7 years at an estimated cost of CU350,000. The entity is required under IAS 16 to allocate the amount initially recognised to its significant parts, and therefore it determines that it shall: • Regard the roof as a significant part with a cost of CU250,000 and a useful life of 10 years; • Regard the internal partitioning as a significant part with a cost of CU350,000 and a useful life of 7 years; and • The remaining cost of CU3.4 million should be allocated to the building component with a useful life of 25 years. This parts approach is illustrated by International Consolidated Airlines Group, S.A. in Practical example 3-3 below and by Skanska AB in Practical example 5-1 (see section 5.2 below). Practical example 3-3: International Consolidated Airlines Group, S.A. (2023) Spain Annual Report and Accounts 2023 [extract] Financial Statements [extract] Notes to the consolidated financial statements [extract] ## Significant accounting policies [extract] Property, plant and equipment [extract] In addition to the requirement to componentise for depreciation purposes, IAS 16 requires entities to derecognise an existing part when it is replaced, regardless of whether it has been depreciated separately. The accounting for subsequent expenditure on replacement of parts is discussed further at 3.3.1 below. 3.3 Initial and subsequent expenditure IAS 16 makes no distinction in principle between the initial costs of acquiring an asset and any subsequent expenditure relating to it. In both cases, any and all expenditure has to meet the recognition criteria and be expensed in profit or loss if it does not. [IAS 16.10]. Extract from IAS 16 An entity evaluates under this recognition principle all its property, plant and equipment costs at the time they are incurred. These costs include costs incurred initially to acquire or construct an item of property, plant and equipment and costs incurred subsequently to add to, replace part of, or service it. The cost of an item of property, plant and equipment may include costs incurred relating to leases of assets that are used to construct, add to, replace part of or service an item of property, plant and equipment, such as depreciation of right-of-use assets. Property, plant and equipment The standard draws a distinction between servicing and more major expenditures. Day-to-day servicing, by which is meant the repair and maintenance of PP&E that largely comprises labour costs, consumables and other minor parts, should be recognised in profit or loss as incurred. [IAS 16.12]. However, if the expenditure involves replacing a significant part of the asset, this part should be capitalised as part of the PP&E, if the recognition criteria are met. The carrying amount of the part that has been replaced should be derecognised (see section 3.3.1 and section 7). [IAS 16.13]. Examples of the treatment of major maintenance expenditure are shown in Practical example 3-4 and Practical example 3-5 below (see section 3.3.2). Further guidance on the accounting for major inspections and overhauls is provided at 3.3.2. Practical example 3-4: Akzo Nobel N.V. (2023) The Netherlands Akzo Nobel Report 2023 FINANCIAL STATEMENTS [extract] NOTES TO THE CONSOLIDATED FINANCIAL STATEMENTS [extract] Note 1: SUMMARY OF MATERIAL ACCOUNTING POLICIES [extract] Property, plant and equipment (Note 11) [extract] … 3.3.1 Subsequent expenditure on replacement of parts When discussing subsequent expenditure, IAS 16 identifies two particular types of ‘parts’ of assets. The first is an item that requires replacement at regular intervals during the life of the asset such as relining a furnace after a specified number of hours of use, or replacing the interiors of an aircraft (e.g., seats and galleys) several times during the life of the airframe. The second type involves less frequently recurring replacements, such as replacing the interior walls of a building. IAS 16 does not state that replacement expenditures necessarily qualify for recognition. Rather, it says that an entity recognises the cost of replacing part of such an item in the carrying amount of an item of PP&E when that cost is incurred, if the recognition criteria are met. Some of its examples, such as aircraft interiors, are clearly best treated as separate assets as they have a useful life different from that of the asset of which they are part. With the other examples, such as interior walls, it may be less clear why they meet the recognition criteria. However, replacing internal walls or similar expenditures may extend the useful life of a building while upgrading machinery may increase its capacity, improve the quality of its output, or reduce operating costs. Hence, this type of expenditure may give rise to future economic benefits. Entities are required to derecognise the carrying amount of an existing part when it is replaced, regardless of whether it has been depreciated separately, and the standard allows the carrying value of the part that has been replaced to be estimated, if necessary: “If it is not practicable for an entity to determine the carrying amount of the replaced part, it may use the cost of the replacement as an indication of what the cost of the replaced part was at the time it was acquired or constructed.” [IAS 16.70] As such, if the entity has no better information than the cost of the replacement part, it is permitted to use a depreciated replacement cost basis to calculate the amount derecognised in respect of the original asset. As a consequence, an entity may not actually identify the parts of an asset until it incurs the replacement expenditure, as in the following Illustration: Illustration 3-3: Recognition and derecognition of replacement parts An entity buys a piece of machinery with an estimated useful life of ten years for CU10 million. The asset contains two identical pumps, which are assumed to have the same useful life as the machine of which they are a part. After seven years one of the pumps fails and is replaced at a cost of CU200,000. The entity had not identified the pumps as separate parts and does not know the original cost. It uses the cost of the replacement part to estimate the carrying value of the original pump. With the help of the supplier, it estimates that the cost would have been approximately CU170,000 and that this would have a remaining carrying value after seven year’s depreciation of CU51,000. Accordingly, it derecognises CU51,000 and capitalises the cost of the replacement. Property, plant and equipment 3.3.2 Major inspections and overhauls It may be necessary to perform major inspections of an item of PP&E at regular intervals as a condition of continuing to use the asset. In such circumstances, when a major inspection or overhaul is performed, the standard allows a separate part (distinct from physical parts) to be recognised for the cost of such inspections if the recognition criteria are satisfied, regardless of whether any physical parts of the asset are replaced. [IAS 16.14]. The reason for this approach is to maintain a degree of consistency with IAS 37 Provisions, Contingent Liabilities and Contingent Assets which prohibits an entity from making provisions if there are no obligations. Therefore, an entity is prohibited by IAS 37 from making, for example, a provision to overhaul an owned aircraft engine by annually providing for a quarter of the cost for four years and then utilising the provision when the engine is overhauled in the fourth year. [IAS 37 IE Example 11A, IAS 37 IE Example 11B]. Instead, the cost of a major inspection is added to the asset’s cost if the recognition criteria are satisfied and then depreciated over the period until the next major inspection. This approach is illustrated in Practical example 3-5: Practical example 3-5: International Consolidated Airlines Group, S.A. (2023) Spain Annual Report and Accounts 2023 [extract] Financial Statements [extract] Notes to the consolidated financial statements [extract] ## Significant accounting policies [extract] Property, plant and equipment [extract] f Maintenance, repairs and overhaul [extract] ‘Pay-as-you-go’ contracts, as referenced in the above examples, are common in the aerospace industry and refer to comprehensive turbine engine maintenance and overhaul contracts, which are usually based on a fixed fee for each hour flown and include loan engines when required. When a major inspection or overhaul is performed, any amount remaining from the previous inspection (as distinct from physical parts) is derecognised. In the same way as described at 3.3.1 for replacement parts more generally, this process of recognition and derecognition should take place regardless of whether the cost of the previous inspection was identified (and considered a separate part) when the asset was originally acquired or constructed. If the element relating to the inspection had previously been identified, it would have been depreciated between that time and the current overhaul and it’s carrying amount would be known. However, if it had not previously been identified, the carrying amount of the component to be derecognised will need to be estimated. In arriving at this estimate, the standard allows the cost of a future similar inspection to be used as an indication of what the cost of the existing inspection component was when the item was acquired or constructed. [IAS 16.14]. However, an entity is not precluded from reconstructing the carrying amount of the previous inspection (i.e., to estimate the net depreciated carrying value of the previous inspection that will be derecognised) rather than simply using a depreciated replacement cost approach. These principles may need to be considered by entities which are required to undertake major overhauls or redesigns of items of PP&E as a result of the impacts of climate change on their business. To the extent that such activities result in the replacement of asset parts, entities will need to apply the ‘major inspection and overhaul’ principles of IAS 16 in determining the appropriate treatment. Property, plant and equipment Measurement at recognition IAS 16 draws a distinction between measurement at recognition (i.e., the initial recognition of an item of PP&E on acquisition) and measurement after recognition (i.e., the subsequent treatment of the item). Measurement after recognition is discussed in sections 5 and 6 below. The standard states that “an item of property, plant and equipment that qualifies for recognition as an asset shall be measured at its cost”. [IAS 16.15]. What may be included in the cost of an item is discussed below. 4.1 Elements of cost and cost measurement IAS 16 sets out what constitutes the cost of an item of PP&E on its initial recognition: [IAS 16.16] Extract from IAS 16 The cost of an item of property, plant and equipment comprises: (a) its purchase price, including import duties and non-refundable purchase taxes, after deducting trade discounts and rebates. (b) any costs directly attributable to bringing the asset to the location and condition necessary for it to be capable of operating in the manner intended by management. (c) the initial estimate of the costs of dismantling and removing the item and restoring the site on which it is located, the obligation for which an entity incurs either when the item is acquired or as a consequence of having used the item during a particular period for purposes other than to produce inventories during that period. One practical example of (c) above may arise in connection with dilapidation obligations in lease agreements, under which a lessee is obliged to return premises to the landlord in an agreed condition. Arguably, a provision is required whenever the ‘damage’ is incurred. Therefore, if an entity rents a building and constructs a dividing wall to convert the single building into two premises and has an obligation to make good at the end of the lease term, the tenant should immediately provide for the costs of so doing. The ‘other side’ of the provision entry is capitalised and will be amortised over the lease term. This reflects the fact that the cost to the tenant of the leasehold improvement includes both the cost of constructing the dividing wall, but also the resulting costs which the entity will have to incur in the future to restore the property. Dilapidation and other provisions relating to leased assets are discussed in more detail in Chapter 27 at section 6.9. Obligations that arise, for example due to damage caused, as a result of using the asset to produce inventories during a particular period are not capitalised as part of the item of PP&E. These are production costs and are included in the cost of inventories. [IAS 16.18]. Obligations that arise due to general use (for example, costs associated with wear and tear arising from the use of a leasehold building for administrative purposes) are expensed as incurred. 4.1.1 ‘Directly attributable’ costs This is the key issue in the measurement of cost. [IAS 16.16]. The standard gives examples of types of expenditure that are, and are not, considered to be directly attributable: [IAS 16.17, IAS 16.19] Extract from IAS 16 Examples of directly attributable costs are: (a) costs of employee benefits (as defined in IAS 19 Employee Benefits) arising directly from the construction or acquisition of the item of property, plant and equipment; (b) costs of site preparation; (c) initial delivery and handling costs; (d) installation and assembly costs; (e) costs of testing whether the asset is functioning properly (ie assessing whether the technical and physical performance of the asset is such that it is capable of being used in the production or supply of goods or services, for rental to others, or for administrative purposes); and (f) professional fees. Property, plant and equipment Only costs that are directly attributable can be capitalised, and capitalisation must cease when the asset is in the location and condition necessary for it to be capable of operating in the manner intended by management. For further discussion see sections 4.1.3 and 4.1.4. IAS 16 requires the costs of testing to be added to the carrying value of the asset. Directly attributable testing costs comprise testing costs incurred when assessing whether the technical and physical performance of the asset is such that it is capable of being used in the production or supply of goods or services, for rental to others, or for administrative purposes. [IAS 16.17(e)]. IAS 16 does not provide guidance on assessing the point at which an asset is capable of being used in the manner intended, and therefore, judgement may be required in determining the period during which testing costs should be capitalised. The cost of an item of PP&E includes costs incurred relating to leases of assets that are used to construct, add to, or replace part of an item of PP&E, such as depreciation of right-of-use assets, where those lease costs are “directly attributable to bringing the asset to the location and condition necessary for it to be capable of operating in the manner intended by management …”. [IAS 16.10, IAS 16.16]. When, for example, an entity leases real estate (land or building) in relation to the construction of real estate or leasehold improvements that meet the definition of PP&E, the entity needs to determine whether the depreciation cost of the right-of-use asset represents a cost that is directly attributable to bringing the asset to a condition necessary for it to be capable of operating in a manner intended by management. If the entity concludes this is the case, the depreciation expense incurred in relation to the right-of-use asset for the lease during the construction period would be capitalised as part of the construction cost of the item of PP&E. [IAS 16.10, IAS 16.16(b)]. Depreciation of a right-of-use asset begins at commencement of the lease, which may be before the completed asset is available for use in circumstances in which real estate or leasehold improvements are being constructed. This differs from the requirement in respect of PP&E, where depreciation of the asset begins when it is available for use in the manner intended by management (see further discussion in section 5.5 below). Where an entity concludes that the depreciation cost of the right-of-use asset does not represent a directly attributable cost of the asset, the entity should expense the costs as incurred. Examples of such considerations are shown in Illustration 4-1 and Illustration 4-2 below. How we see it The cost of an item of PP&E includes the depreciation of right-of-use assets, where that depreciation expense is directly attributable to bringing the item to the location and condition necessary for it to be capable of operating in the manner intended by management. Determining whether the depreciation expense associated with a right-of-use asset is directly attributable requires judgement, taking into account all relevant facts and circumstances. Certain types of expenditure are not directly attributable costs associated with an item of PP&E and therefore, cannot be recognised as part of the cost of an asset. This should be considered in assessing whether a lease cost is included in the cost of an item of PP&E. Some specific examples of ineligible costs are discussed further at section 4.1.3 below. Illustration 4-1: Depreciation charge for right-of-use asset - owner-occupied building On 1 January 20X0, an entity enters into a lease of land for a term of 40 years. The entity intends to construct a building on the land and plans to occupy the building itself once construction is completed. The entity must obtain planning permission for the building, and this process takes three years. Therefore, the land lease commences on 1 January 20X0. Construction of the building begins on 1 January 20X3 and is completed two years later, on 1 January 20X5. The entity applies the cost model for its PP&E. The entity needs to determine whether the depreciation cost of the right-of-use asset represents a cost that is directly attributable to bringing the asset to a condition necessary for it to be capable of operating in the manner intended by management as specified in IAS 16. [IAS 16.16(b)]. In this scenario the depreciation charge during the construction period is an unavoidable cost of developing the property, because without the lease of the land no construction of the building can occur. Therefore, the depreciation charge related to the right-of-use asset for the land lease incurred during the period from 1 January 20X3 to 31 December 20X4 is capitalised. Thereafter, the depreciation charge is recognised in expense. Depreciation in the period prior to 1 January 20X3 is expensed as incurred as depreciation during that time is not directly attributable to the construction of the asset itself. Property, plant and equipment Illustration 4-2: Depreciation charge for right-of-use asset - leasehold improvements On 1 January 20X0, an entity enters into a 10-year lease of a building. To make the building suitable for its requirements, the entity makes leasehold improvements beginning on the commencement date of the lease. From lease commencement, the entity cannot begin its intended operations until the work to construct the leasehold improvements is completed. On 1 April 20X0, the building is available to begin the entity’s operations. Considerable judgement may be required in examples such as this one, where an entity is required to assess whether the depreciation expense incurred on a leased building is directly attributable to the cost of associated leasehold improvements. Factors an entity needs to consider may include the condition of the leased asset prior to making the leasehold improvements (for example, whether it is merely a shell building which cannot be operated), the nature of the asset prior to the leasehold improvements and the significance of the improvements to be made (for example, whether the changes are relatively minor or superficial, or whether they involve a full repurposing of the asset to enable its use). In addition, IAS 16 is clear that certain ‘start up’ costs, which include ‘the costs of opening a new facility’ are not costs of an item of PP&E. Therefore, there may also be significant judgement in determining whether the depreciation should be viewed as a ‘start- up’ cost and expensed as incurred on that basis. If, having made this assessment, the entity concludes that the depreciation is directly attributable to bringing the asset to a condition necessary for it to be capable of operating in the manner intended by management, the entity would capitalise the depreciation expense as part of construction costs. Otherwise, the entity should expense the costs as incurred. Once the entity begins operations, the depreciation charge related to right-of-use asset is expensed as incurred. 4.1.2 Borrowing costs Borrowing costs must be capitalised in respect of certain qualifying assets if those assets are measured at cost. A qualifying asset is “an asset that necessarily takes a substantial period of time to get ready for its intended use or sale”. [IAS 23.5]. Assets that are ready for their intended use or sale when acquired are not qualifying assets. [IAS 23.7]. Therefore, an entity will capitalise borrowing costs on a self-constructed item of PP&E if it meets the criteria in IAS 23 Borrowing Costs. [IAS 16.22]. Further considerations in respect of self- built assets are discussed in section 4.1.5 below. The treatment of borrowing costs is discussed separately in Chapter 22. Entities are not required to capitalise borrowing costs in respect of assets that are measured at fair value. This includes revalued PP&E which is measured at fair value through Other Comprehensive Income (‘OCI’). Generally, an item of PP&E within the scope of IAS 16 will only be carried at revalued amount once construction is completed, so capitalisation of borrowing costs will have ceased (see section 4.1.4 below). This is not necessarily the case with investment property in the course of construction (see # Chapter 20 at section 2.5). The cost of an asset, before adopting a policy of revaluation, will include capitalised borrowing costs. Thereafter, IAS 23 does not prohibit capitalisation of eligible borrowing costs for assets carried at fair value as a matter of accounting policy. [IAS 23.4(a), IAS 23.BC4]. To the extent that entities choose to capitalise borrowing costs in respect of assets still in the course of construction that are carried at fair value, the methods allowed by IAS 23 should be followed. For disclosure purposes, an entity will still need to monitor the carrying amount of PP&E measured under the revaluation model, including those borrowing costs that would have been recognised, had such an asset been carried under the cost model (see section 8.2 below). 4.1.3 Start up costs, administration and other general overheads Administration and other general overhead costs are not costs of an item of PP&E. This means that employee costs not related to a specific asset, such as site selection activities and general management time do not qualify for capitalisation. Entities are also not allowed to recognise so-called ‘start up costs’ as part of the item of PP&E. These include costs related to opening a new facility, introducing a new product or service (including costs of advertising and promotional activities), conducting business in a new territory or with a new class of customer (including costs of staff training) and similar items. [IAS 16.19]. These costs should be accounted for in the same way as similar costs incurred as part of the entity’s on-going activities (in general, expensed as incurred). Property, plant and equipment 4.1.4 Cessation of capitalisation Cost recognition ceases once an item of PP&E is in the location and condition necessary for it to be capable of operating in the manner intended by management. This will usually be the date of practical completion of the physical asset. IAS 16, therefore prohibits the recognition of relocation and reorganisation costs, costs incurred in using the asset or during the run up to full use once the asset is ready to be used, and any initial operating losses. [IAS 16.20]. An entity is not precluded from continuing to capitalise costs during an initial commissioning period that is necessary for running in machinery or testing equipment. In contrast, no new costs should be capitalised if the asset is fully operational but is not yet achieving its targeted output, usage or profitability because demand is still building up, for example in a new hotel that initially has high room vacancies or a partially let investment property. In these cases, the asset is clearly in the location and condition necessary for it to be capable of operating in the manner intended by management. Entities need to identify the point at which major assets are considered to be in the location and condition necessary for it to be capable of operating in the manner intended by management and apply that policy consistently. 4.1.5 Self-built assets If an asset is self-built by the entity, the same general principles apply as for an acquired asset. The costs of abnormal amounts of wasted resources, whether labour, materials or other resources, are not included in the cost of such self-built assets. If the same type of asset is made for resale by the business, the cost of such an asset to be held as property, plant and equipment is usually the same as the cost of constructing that asset for sale, i.e., without including any internal profit element but including attributable overheads in accordance with IAS 2 (see Chapter 23). IAS 23, discussed in Chapter 22, contains criteria relating to the recognition of any interest as a component of the carrying amount of a self-built item of PP&E. [IAS 16.22]. Kendrion N.V. provides an example of an accounting policy for self-built assets. Practical example 4-1: Kendrion N.V. (2023) The Netherlands Annual Integrated Report 2023 [extract] FINANCIAL STATEMENTS [extract] NOTES TO THE CONSOLIDATED FINANCIAL STATEMENTS [extract] Significant accounting policies [extract] (c) Property, plant and equipment [extract] (i) Owned assets [extract] … 4.1.6 Deferred payment IAS 16 specifically precludes the capitalisation of hidden credit charges as part of the cost of an item of PP&E, so the cost of an item of PP&E is its cash price equivalent at the recognition date. This means that if payment is made in some other manner, the cost to be capitalised is the normal cash price. Thus, if the payment terms are extended beyond ‘normal’ credit terms, the cost to be recognised must be the cash price equivalent and any difference between the cash price equivalent and the total payment must be treated and recognised as an interest expense over the period of credit unless such interest is capitalised in accordance with IAS 23 (see section 4.1.2 above). [IAS 16.23]. Right-of-use assets under IFRS 16 are discussed in Chapter 24. Assets partly paid for by government grants are discussed in Chapter 25. 4.1.7 Land and buildings to be redeveloped It is common for property developers to acquire land with an existing building where the planned redevelopment necessitates the demolition of that building and its replacement with a new building that is to be held to earn rentals or will be owner occupied. Property, plant and equipment How we see it IAS 16 requires that the building and land be classified as two separate items (see section 5.4.2 below). In our view it is appropriate, if the existing building is unusable or likely to be demolished by any party acquiring it, that the entire or a large part of the purchase price be allocated to the land. Similarly, subsequent demolition costs should be treated as being attributable to the cost of the land. Entities replacing an existing building with a building held to earn rentals or capital appreciation or a building to be sold in the ordinary course of their business will deal with the land and buildings under IAS 40 (see Chapter 20 at section 4.2.1) or IAS 2 (see Chapter 23 at section 4.2.2), respectively. Owner-occupiers may also take the decision to replace existing buildings which they have previously occupied with new facilities for their own use, to rent to others or to be held for capital appreciation or sold in the ordinary course of the entity’s business. How we see it Here the consequences are different and the carrying amount of the existing building cannot be rolled into the costs of the new development. In this case, the land and buildings have already previously been separately recognised as assets under IAS 16. The existing building must be depreciated over its remaining useful life to reduce the carrying amount of the asset to its residual value (presumably nil) at the point at which it is demolished. Consideration will have to be given as to whether the asset is impaired in accordance with IAS 36 (see section 5.7 below). Many properties do not directly generate independent cash inflows (i.e., they are part of a cash-generating unit) and reducing the useful life will not necessarily lead to an impairment of the cash- generating unit (meaning the property itself may initially be shielded from impairment). However, by the time the asset has been designated for demolition it may no longer be part of a cash-generating unit (see # Chapter 21 at sections 2.1.3 and 3). 4.1.8 Transfers of assets from customers An entity may be entitled to consideration in the form of goods, services or other non-cash consideration (e.g., PP&E), in exchange for transferring goods or services to a customer. Examples include: • A supplier who receives a contribution to the development costs of specific tooling equipment from another manufacturer to whom the supplier will sell parts, using that specific tooling equipment under a supply agreement. • Suppliers of utilities who receive items of PP&E from customers that are used to connect them to a network through which they will receive ongoing services (e.g., electricity, gas, water or telephone services). A typical arrangement is one in which a builder or individual householder must pay for power cables, pipes, or other connections. • In outsourcing arrangements, the existing assets are often contributed to the service provider, or the customer must pay for assets, or both. This raises questions about recognising assets for which the entity has not paid. In what circumstances should the entity recognise these assets, how should it be measured on initial recognition and how is the ‘other side’ of the accounting entry dealt with? Is it revenue and if so, how and over what period is it recognised? There are a number of potential answers to this and, unsurprisingly, there is variety in practice. When an entity (i.e., the seller or vendor) receives, or expects to receive, non-cash consideration from a customer in relation to a revenue contract that is within the scope of IFRS 15 Revenue from Contracts with Customers, the fair value of the non-cash consideration is included in the transaction price. [IFRS 15.66]. The IFRS 15 requirements regarding non-cash consideration are discussed in detail in Chapter 28 at section 5.6. The Basis for Conclusions on IFRS 15 states that “once recognised, any asset arising from the non-cash consideration would be measured and accounted for in accordance with other relevant requirements …” (e.g., IAS 16). [IFRS 15.BC253]. As such, the fair value measured in accordance with IFRS 15 would be the deemed cost of the item of PP&E on initial recognition. Property, plant and equipment 4.1.9 Variable and contingent consideration The purchase price of an item of PP&E might include variable or contingent amounts. Transactions involving variable or contingent consideration are often very complex and payment may depend on a number of factors. Examples include variable or contingent payments based on an index or rate, variable or contingent payments which depend upon the purchasers’ future actions, variable or contingent payments related to the condition of the asset, or variable or contingent payments related to the asset’s performance. Some arrangements may contain more than one source of variability. Further complexity can also arise when arrangements are akin to profit sharing agreements which are not addressed in any standard. The topic of variable payments for an asset purchase was discussed by the IFRS IC between 2011 and 2016, and an agenda decision was published in March 2016. The IFRS IC’s discussions considered both the accounting at the date of purchasing the asset and subsequently, and distinguished between those variable payments that depend on the purchaser’s future activity and those that do not. The IFRS IC determined that the issue was too broad for it to address within the confines of existing standards and recommended that the IASB address the accounting for variable payments comprehensively. In May 2016, the IASB tentatively agreed that this issue would be included in the research pipeline between 2017 and 2021. At the time of writing this is not listed as an active research project on the IASB’s work plan. A critical step in analysing the accounting for the purchase of an item of PP&E in an arrangement involving variable or contingent consideration is to fully understand the contractual arrangements, understanding the nature of the contingent or variable payments. This includes developing a full understanding of whether those contingent payments are linked to the initial value of the asset, or to its future performance, whether the contingency or variability is within or outside of the purchasers’ control and whether the variable payment is a payment for some other goods or services from the seller. In some cases, the contract may establish an obligation for the contingent payment at the outset, whilst in others it might be determined that the arrangement is an executory contract, and the liability to make the contingent payments is only recognised later once either party fulfils its obligations under the contract. The two key issues with regards to the accounting for variable consideration in an asset purchase are 1) whether and when to recognise a liability for that variable consideration, and 2) whether subsequent changes to the estimate of variable or contingent consideration should be accounted for as part of the cost of the acquired asset. Looking at the first question of when a liability to make variable or contingent payments would be recognised, if the arrangement is not considered to be an executory contract, one consideration is whether those payments are dependent on the entity’s future activity, or not. IAS 32.25 stipulates that where the contingency is outside the control of both the issuer and the holder, a contingent obligation to deliver cash meets the definition of a financial liability (see Chapter 43 at section 4.3). Therefore, if the obligation to make the variable payment does not depend on the acquiring entity’s future activity, it would seem appropriate to recognise a financial liability in respect of the contingent amounts. [IAS 32.25]. However, it is acknowledged that whilst this view was tentatively agreed during the course of the IFRS IC discussions on the topic, ultimately, this was not clarified or confirmed as part of the final agenda decision. Furthermore, the final agenda decision notes that there are questions about the accounting for variable payments subsequent to the purchase of the asset, and in making that statement the IFRS IC did not distinguish between the different types of variable payments. How we see it In light of the discussions held by the IFRS IC and the absence of clear guidance in the final agenda decision, entities will need to develop and apply an accounting policy in respect of whether and when the obligation to make contingent payments is recognised as a liability. In doing so, entities will need to apply the hierarchy in IAS 8. The policy selected should result in information that is relevant and reliable in the particular circumstances and must be consistently applied to similar transactions. Different payments may have different types of variability. Therefore, it might be appropriate for an entity to have different policies for dissimilar transactions. If information about the selected accounting policy is expected to be material to users of the financial statements, that accounting policy information should be disclosed. ## Contents - 1. Variability arises from exchange rates, time value of money, or embedded derivatives - Changes in the ## Contents - 2. Variability is directly linked to the quality or quantity of the asset(s) received - In these cases the initial ## Contents - 3. The variability in the arrangement does not fall under (1) or (2) above and an accounting policy needs Property, plant and equipment As described above, there is no explicit guidance on the treatment of variable or contingent consideration in the acquisition of an asset and there is diversity in practice for these types of arrangements. Therefore, examples of some accounting policies an entity may want to consider regarding initial recognition of the asset include the following: • The cost of the asset on initial recognition includes the fair value of all variable or contingent consideration at the date of acquisition. A corresponding liability is recognised for the same amount. • The cost of the asset on initial recognition excludes any variable or contingent payments. Accordingly, no liability is recognised for those contingent or variable amounts. The second question that needs to be addressed is how the entity must account for subsequent payments and changes to the estimate of variable or contingent consideration. Depending on the facts and circumstances of the arrangement an entity may determine that: carrying amount of a recognised liability that arise from exchange rates are accounted for in accordance with IAS 21 The Effects of Changes in Foreign Exchange Rates and IFRIC 22 Foreign Currency Transactions and Advance Consideration, while the time value of money and embedded derivatives are accounted for in accordance with IFRS 9 Financial Instruments; recognition of the asset and liability were based on an accounting estimate that is subsequently updated in accordance with IAS 8.37; or to be developed to address it. In the latter case, an entity will need to apply the requirements of IAS 8 to develop an accounting policy that it should apply consistently to all similar transactions. The accounting policy developed will need to address whether the change in the estimate of variable or contingent consideration should be capitalised as part of the cost of the asset or expensed. This determination is usually based on the reason for the contingent payment and entities should consider the substance of the arrangement in determining the appropriate treatment. It is only appropriate for subsequent changes to a recognised liability to be recognised as part of the cost of the asset to the extent that it relates to future economic benefits embodied in the asset initially received. For example, if the contingency or variability is viewed as being linked to the quality or quantity of the asset initially received. Variability arising due to other factors, such as its use over time, would generally be expensed. How we see it Until this matter is resolved by the IASB, where the accounting for the variability or contingency is not prescribed in another standard, an entity should adopt and apply a consistent accounting policy in relation to whether subsequent payments or changes to the measurement of a recognised liability to make contingent or variable payments are included as part of the cost of an acquired asset or expensed as incurred, in accordance with the hierarchy in IAS 8. An entity should exercise judgement in developing and consistently applying an accounting policy that results in information that is relevant and reliable in the particular circumstances. If information about the selected accounting policy is expected to be material to users of the financial statements, that accounting policy information should be disclosed. 4.1.10 Acquisition of a group of assets The purchase price of an individual item of PP&E may be an allocation of the price paid for a group of assets. If an entity acquires a group of assets that do not comprise a business (‘the group’), such a transaction is outside the scope of IFRS 3 Business Combinations, but nonetheless, IFRS 3 sets out specific principles to be applied in order to allocate the entire cost to individual items (see Chapter 10 at section 2.2.2). These principles differ from the approach required for a business combination. In such cases, the acquirer should identify and recognise the individual identifiable assets acquired (including those assets that meet the definition of, and recognition criteria for, intangible assets in IAS 38) and liabilities assumed. The cost of the group should be allocated to the individual identifiable assets and liabilities based on their relative fair values at the date of purchase. Such a transaction or event does not give rise to goodwill. [IFRS 3.2(b)]. Property, plant and equipment Frequently asked questions Question 4-1: How does an entity account for the acquisition of a group of assets that does not constitute a business? In November 2017, the IFRS IC issued an agenda decision that clarified how to account for a group of assets that does not constitute a business. The agenda decision more specifically addressed how to allocate the transaction price to the identifiable assets acquired and liabilities assumed when the sum of the individual fair values of the identifiable assets and liabilities is different from the transaction price, and the group includes identifiable assets and liabilities initially measured both at cost and at an amount other than cost. The IFRS IC concluded that a reasonable reading of the requirements in IFRS 3.2(b) (as described above) on the acquisition of the group, is that there are two possible approaches.1 These two approaches are discussed in detail in Chapter 10 at section 2.2.2. See also similar discussion relating to investment property in Chapter 20 at section 4.1.1. 4.1.11 Dismantling and restoration costs If an asset is used to produce inventories during a particular period, the costs of obligations that are incurred during that period to dismantle, remove or restore the site on which such asset has been located are not capitalised as part of the item of PP&E. Instead, these are costs of producing inventory and are dealt with in accordance with IAS 2 (see Chapter 23). [IAS 16.18]. Site restoration costs and other environmental restoration and similar costs arising from the construction or commissioning of an asset must be estimated and capitalised at initial recognition, even if the expenditure will only be incurred at the end of the item’s life. In addition, in some cases an obligation for such costs may arise later in the asset’s life, for example as a result of changes in legislation. Such costs would also be capitalised as part of the cost of the item of PP&E. The obligations are calculated in accordance with IAS 37 and IFRIC 1 Changes in Existing Decommissioning, Restoration and Similar Liabilities (see section 4.3 below and Chapter 27 at section 6.3). [IAS 16.16]. This is illustrated in Practical example 4-2 below. Practical example 4-2: **E.ON SE (2023)** Germany Integrated Annual Report 2023 [extract] Consolidated Financial Statements [extract] Notes [extract] (1) Summary of Significant Accounting Policies [extract] Property, Plant and Equipment [extract] … IFRIC Update, November 2017, available on the IFRS Foundation’s website.
